A local follow-on adds conservative YAML maps and scalar sequences; TOML tables, dotted keys and simple scalar values; and XML 1.0 elements, leaf text and attributes with predefined and numeric single-character entities. Each emits fields with key paths, UTF-16 source spans, decoded values and a credential flag. The flag is inherited from any credential-like ancestor key or a case-insensitive `name`/`key` plus `value` sibling, and it is set for token-shaped usernames. Comments are listed, never dropped. Malformed or over-budget input is a whole-input `FAILURE`; unsupported YAML flow, anchor, tag, block, multi-document and quoted-key constructs; TOML arrays, inline tables, dates, multiline strings and quoted keys; and XML DTDs, external entities, namespaces, CDATA, processing instructions and mixed content are wholly opaque. Unrecognized dotenv/INI lines and over-long or unterminated log values are opaque (`PARTIAL`); log parsing is fields-only. YAML/TOML line iteration and XML scanning are bounded by input size, depth, field, comment, path-element and XML node caps; TOML also caps the stored and computed path-ID expansion, and XML 1.0 character validity is checked on source and replacements. Credential inheritance can over-flag, for example any key with non-ASCII letters or a `key`/`pass` suffix; that trades utility for safety. `rewriteFieldValues` re-encodes replacements for the parsed syntax. It refuses sources with comments unless told to remove them, refuses ambiguous duplicate-key JSON, and re-parses to verify every key and value, failing rather than passing the original through. Known limits: log values stop at `,`/`;`/whitespace. An explicit bounded time budget is now enforced in concert with the size, depth, field, comment, path and expansion caps: `ParseBudget` carries a validated `maxTimeMs` (default 2 000 ms, ceiling `MAX_PARSE_TIME_MS` = 60 000 ms) that is measured against a **host-owned** monotonic clock passed separately from the budget, so a serialized caller assertion is never a trusted clock and payload text can neither supply, disable nor forge one. Every supported format charges bounded scan and recursion steps (per line, per JSON value and string, per URL/query/connection pair, per YAML/TOML path id and named-value sweep, per XML element, attribute, comment and scalar) and samples that clock at most once per 64 charged steps plus once more before any success-shaped result. A parse that exhausts its budget is `FAILURE`/`TIME_BUDGET_EXPIRED` with the original whole input opaque, no field, span, coverage or partial parse; a clock that throws, is non-finite or moves backwards is `FAILURE`/`CLOCK_UNAVAILABLE` on the same terms, because a clock that cannot report elapsed time must not read as time remaining. `rewriteFieldValues` applies the same budget to the source and the round-trip re-parse and returns `TIME_BUDGET_EXPIRED`/`CLOCK_UNAVAILABLE` carrying **no output text**, never the original source or a source-completeness excuse. Honest limits of the cooperative budget: one single bounded primitive (a whole-input character-class or XML-validity sweep, one long string, scalar or regular-expression run) is never interrupted, so the worst overshoot is that primitive plus 64 charged steps; the budget is per parse call, not per request, so a rewrite is bounded by its two parses plus a bounded splice rather than by one window; it does not preempt a hostile `Proxy` trap, a blocking regular expression, or arbitrary same-process user code that never returns to a check point; a host that supplies a frozen or dishonest reading cannot be told apart from a slow host, which is exactly why the clock is a trusted host capability and never budget or payload data; and it composes no v1 classification unit, transforms nothing and authorizes no release. The parsers are consumed by the #6 composition seam (decoded field values and trusted key hints) but are not yet wired to #8 detectors directly or to #13 transformation.

Local #7 follow-on evidence (Node 22.22.3, npm 11.15.0): `npm ci --ignore-scripts --no-fund`, `npm run build`, `npm run typecheck`, focused `node --test test/structured-parsers.test.mjs` (35/35 groups), full `npm test` on retry (366/366 tests) and `git diff --check` passed. Intermittent full runs failed a randomized assertion in the separate egress-sentinel suite; its focused rerun and the full retries passed, and that flakiness is tracked separately. (That historical observation was recorded without captured output: no failing group, assertion or log for those runs exists in this repository, and it is still undiagnosed. It is left exactly as recorded. Later induced-load measurements are described in the #11 paragraphs below and do not identify it.) A first independent review identified inherited XML entity names, credential sibling aliases/casing and uncharged TOML implicit table prefixes; regressions and fixes are in this follow-on, pending rereview. Synthetic tests cover nested and oversized structures, long lines, duplicate keys/attributes, credential ancestors and `name`/`key` plus `value` siblings, escaped/entity source spans, invalid XML characters and entities, comments, unsupported hidden credentials, explicit rewrite refusal and 120 seeded round-trip rewrite cases per format. This is local parser contract evidence, not independent review approval, held-out recall, downstream utility, parser-to-policy wiring or proof of protected egress. A later, independent acceptance audit of the full public #7 body found every functional parser and opaque-release criterion satisfied but recorded expressly that no wall-clock budget exists, so that COMPLETE verdict does not satisfy the issue's explicit size/depth/**token**/**time**/expansion requirement; #7 is therefore kept open for this gap. Note also that the root closure of #6 is bounded normalization and content-type detection only, not deployed enforcement.

Local #7 explicit parser time/deadline budget evidence (Node 22.22.3, npm 10.9.8; worktree `parser-time-budget`, based on `2f1ecab`): `src/structured-parsers.ts` gained one validated `maxTimeMs` limit (default 2 000 ms, ceiling 60 000 ms) measured against a narrow, injectable, host-owned monotonic clock behind a separate fourth `parseStructured` argument, so the serialized budget can never carry a clock (`{ maxTimeMs: 5, clock: ... }` is `INVALID_BUDGET`) and no payload can supply, disable or rewind one; `src/normalized-detection.ts` forwards that same host capability to #7 and refuses a host that is not a usable clock with `INVALID_HOST` before any parse runs. The new behavior tests are [`test/parser-time-budget.test.mjs`](../test/parser-time-budget.test.mjs): eleven groups, every clock injected, no sleep, no busy loop, no CPU stress and **no wall-clock assertion anywhere in the file**. They cover the validated budget shape and the unchanged size/depth/field caps; expiry to a whole-input `FAILURE`/`TIME_BUDGET_EXPIRED` with zero fields for **all nine formats** at two document sizes each, each against a positive control that parses the same document completely with a frozen clock; expiry observed at a bounded scan boundary rather than only after a whole document is read (the clock is sampled exactly twice — start plus the first poll — even for a 1 000-field document of the same format); the final sample that keeps a late-finishing parse from reporting `COMPLETE`/`PARTIAL`; clock rollback, non-finite, non-number, throwing and throwing-after-start closures; a host that is null, primitive, empty, non-function, a throwing accessor or a throwing `Proxy` trap, plus a `Proxy` proving the clock is read exactly once per parse and a time-varying clock that can only fail closed; `INPUT_TOO_LARGE`/`INVALID_TEXT`/`INVALID_INPUT`, malformed, duplicate-key, over-depth and unsupported documents keeping exactly their established codes and spans; a rewrite whose source parse or whose round-trip re-parse exhausts the budget returning `TIME_BUDGET_EXPIRED` with `Object.hasOwn(result,'text') === false`; composition of the time-expired parse as `PARTIAL` with `PARSER_JSON_FAILURE` and `PARSER_JSON_TIME_BUDGET_EXPIRED`, a whole-view opaque location, no parsed-field candidate and the planted value still found by the independent whole-text scan; an unresolved classification denied `UNRESOLVED_CLASSIFICATION`, a resolved `SECRET` claim denied `UNSAFE_TREATMENT` under a would-keep rule and only `REMOVE` selectable for an external profile; a controlled final-byte `checkEgress` BLOCK with `release === undefined` on the exact outbound bytes of the document whose parse had just expired, with a same-bytes-minus-the-planted-value ALLOW positive control and no value in the reported findings; and bounded clock-read amortisation over every format. The first behavioral suite was red before the fix: extracted with `git archive 2f1ecab` into a disposable directory and given **only** a link shim (the `MAX_PARSE_TIME_MS` export and the `maxTimeMs` default it compares), the same file fails 10 of its 11 groups — the one that passes is the control group asserting that existing refusals are unchanged. Bounded work was measured once, on this idle host, at the 1 MiB default input cap with the real clock: the worst of the **eight** large synthetic documents measured parses in 82 ms against 77 ms for the same cases on the pre-change tree (per case 4.1/54.1/82.2/27.5/37.2/4.0/7.7/24.7 ms against 4.2/47.6/76.7/25.8/26.0/3.3/6.5/23.9 ms), i.e. the amortized sampling is a single-digit-percent cost inside run-to-run noise; these are local observations, **not** an SLA, and a loaded or slower host has proportionally less headroom. **That paragraph's headroom figure was wrong and an independent review of this head blocked on it.** The eight-document set omitted the URL shape with one large component, whose per-code-point percent-decoding cost the sampler never sees: at the same default cap a 1 048 576-byte URL parsed in **1 809 ms** on this head, i.e. **1.1x** the 2 000 ms default, not 24x under it, so a legal default-budget payload sat a few percent from flipping to whole-input opaque on a slightly slower host. The measured sampling cost above was accurate; the default and the headroom claim were not. No existing size, depth, field, token or timing threshold and no fixture was raised or retuned anywhere in this change. Threats addressed within the tested surface: a parser denial of service that the size/depth/field caps do not bound (work that is linear in bytes but not in the host's speed), a caller that asserts its own remaining time, and a degraded or dishonest clock. Not claimed and not done: the budget is cooperative, per parse call rather than per request, and does not preempt a hostile `Proxy` trap, a blocking regular expression or arbitrary same-process user code; no adapter calls `parseStructured` or `rewriteFieldValues` at a send point, so nothing here is deployed interception, and the egress sentinel's own opaque-content binding remains #6's `UNINSPECTED_CONTENT` — the controlled release attempt above exercises the existing gate, it is not a new #7-specific interception; there is still no #13 transformation, no v1 classification-unit merge, no held-out recall evidence and no end-to-end utility measurement. The budget was not tuned against the acceptance audit or any held-out material, which is not a suite here; no proposed decision 010, #65 taxonomy or #66 multidimensional design was adopted, and classification v1 and policy contracts are unchanged. This is local contract evidence only: independent exact-head review of this head, remote CI, and the rest of #7's open scope remain outstanding, so #7 stays **open**. **Qualified in place 2026-10-02:** [#7](https://github.com/Marcus-Levin/hylja/issues/7) was subsequently closed at its bounded structured-parser scope by the post-merge acceptance audit, read back as completed; [#13](https://github.com/Marcus-Levin/hylja/issues/13) transformation, [#19](https://github.com/Marcus-Levin/hylja/issues/19) at a real send point, v1 classification merging, held-out recall and end-to-end utility all remain unimplemented and unclaimed.

Correction after the independent review that blocked `79fbd2b` (finding F1, plus F2–F4). The implementation stayed fail-closed throughout; the defect was in the authoritative record and in a default that left no usable headroom for a legal payload, so the fix is narrow and stays inside the same module and the same contracts.

**F1 — the default and its headroom.** `percentDecode` encoded a URL component one code point at a time, allocating a `TextEncoder` result per character into a `number[]`; at the 1 MiB cap that cost 1 809–1 946 ms for one URL component, which is what the omitted shape measured. Rather than raise the default to hide it, the decoder now encodes bounded literal **slices** and is **charged per slice and per escape**: a 1 048 576-byte URL component parses in **7–8 ms** (1 796–1 809 ms at `79fbd2b`), and a fully escaped one in **35 ms** (55 ms at `79fbd2b`, now without a regular expression or a slice per escape). The default stays **2 000 ms**. A 15-shape sweep at the 1 MiB default cap, one measurement per shape, now has its worst case at **106 ms** (`XML one huge text`) — **18.9x** margin — against 1 809 ms (`URL short path segments`) and a 1.1x margin at `79fbd2b` on the same shapes in the same session. Per shape, this head against `79fbd2b`: YAML one huge quoted scalar 93/96 ms, TOML one huge basic string 92/95, XML one huge text 106/93, CONNSTR one huge braced 69/93, JSON one huge string 80/69, JSON many containers 67/69, DOTENV 200k lines 59/57, URL many query pairs 61/41, INI one line 2/3, XML 4 096 attributes 2/1, LOG one huge line 18/18, URL short path segments 7/1 809, URL one huge query value 8/1 796, URL huge percent-encoded 35/55 ms. The URL component rows are the change; the rest is run-to-run noise on a shared host, not a claimed improvement. **Charging the decode in slices also means a long component is now observed while it is decoded**: with an injected clock, a 1 MiB URL component is stopped mid-decode with `FAILURE`/`TIME_BUDGET_EXPIRED` and no field, where at `79fbd2b` the same case completed because the whole decode was one uninterruptible primitive. That is the behavioral regression for F1, and it is deterministic — no sleep, no wall-clock assertion.

**F2 — uninterruptible primitive cost, quantified.** The cost of the largest single primitive the budget cannot interrupt scales with `maxInputUnits`, not with `maxTimeMs`. At the 16 MiB ceiling with the tightest legal budget (`{ maxInputUnits: 16777216, maxTimeMs: 1 }`), measured on this head: URL short path segments 43 ms, URL one huge query value 59 ms, URL fully escaped 58 ms, LOG one huge line 272 ms, CONNSTR one huge braced 1 200 ms, YAML one huge scalar 1 780 ms, XML one huge text 1 862 ms, JSON one huge string 1 926 ms, TOML one huge basic string 2 192 ms. The three URL rows were 32 400 ms at `79fbd2b` for the same shapes; the remaining rows are the pre-existing whole-value scan of one scalar or text node and are unchanged by this work. A caller that opts into the 16 MiB input ceiling is accepting up to about 2.2 s of uninterruptible work per primitive, whatever the time budget says.

**F3 — host capability type and call.** `ParseHost.now` is now **required** rather than optional, matching what both consumers actually enforce, and the snapshot is invoked **bound to the host that owns it**, so a method-style `now() { return this.t }` reads its own state instead of resolving `this` to the deadline and failing closed as a surprise. The shape check is now one exported function, `isParseHost`, used by both the parser and the #6 composition seam, so the two cannot drift.

**F4 — clock unit contract.** The unit is **milliseconds** by contract with the host. A host reporting seconds makes every elapsed span read as about 0, so the budget never fires; no in-process check can tell that from a fast parse, so this is a trusted-host obligation, stated in the module contract and **pinned by a test** that shows the seconds host is shape-accepted and the budget does not fire, rather than left as an implicit trap.

**Behaviour preserved and proved.** The decode rewrite is differential against a characterization copy of the previous algorithm kept in the test file: fixed shapes (empty, plain, `+`, every escape boundary, `%`/`%z`/`%4` truncation, invalid UTF-8 `%C3%28`, `%C0%80`, `%FF`, `%00`, astral, non-hex neighbours, inherited property names, runs straddling the decode slice from both sides, 3 000 consecutive escapes, 2 000 escape/literal pairs) plus 3 000 generated shapes across both decoder modes — query with `+` as space and userinfo without — compared for the decoded value, the failure reason and the absence of any field on a decode error. The comparison pins the decoded byte stream and the failure code; the internal `Malformed.at` offset is not part of `ParseResult`, so it is not asserted and a change to it would be unobservable through the public API rather than silently tolerated. The equivalence group passes at `79fbd2b` too, where the reviewed head still *is* the reference algorithm, which is correct for a guard on a new optimisation; it was confirmed non-vacuous by mutation (dropping the `+` conversion fails it). Both limits of that check are recorded rather than smoothed over: the single mutation exercises only the `+` path and says nothing about the astral path, and the 349 515-escape figure belongs to the mid-decode group's escaped 1 MiB URL — a parse-complete control, not a differential comparison. F5 below is what that gap cost.

**Red before green, and what the tests actually pin.** Against `79fbd2b` plus a link shim exporting only `isParseHost` (no behavioural change), the file fails **2 of 13** groups: the bound host clock (F3) and the mid-decode charging (F1/F2). Against base `2f1ecab` plus the earlier shim it still cannot link, so the time-budget groups never ran there; the pre-existing 10-of-11 red measurement recorded above is unchanged. Mutation checks on the final source, each a single edit, confirm the new groups are load-bearing rather than decorative: removing the final sample before a success-shaped result fails 8 groups; sampling the clock at every step instead of every 64 fails the amortisation and mid-decode groups; charging a whole component as one primitive fails the mid-decode group; not charging escapes fails it; re-reading `host.now` on every sample instead of snapshotting it once fails the single-read group. One mutation is **not** caught and is recorded rather than hidden: reporting `BAD_PERCENT` at a different internal offset passes, because that offset never reaches `ParseResult`.

**Scope and gates unchanged.** No adapter calls `parseStructured` or `rewriteFieldValues` at a send point, so nothing here is deployed interception and the egress sentinel's opaque binding remains #6's `UNINSPECTED_CONTENT`; the root closure of #6 is bounded normalization and content-type detection only. All size, depth, field, comment, path, node and expansion caps, format selection, spans, key paths, syntax tags, `highRisk` and the fixture guards are untouched, no dependency was added, and no existing size or timing budget, threshold or fixture was raised to accommodate any run. No proposed decision 010, #65 taxonomy or #66 multidimensional design was adopted and no v1 classification or policy contract changed. Independent review of this corrected head, remote CI, and the rest of #7's open scope remain outstanding, so #7 stays **open**. **Qualified in place 2026-10-02:** [#7](https://github.com/Marcus-Levin/hylja/issues/7) was subsequently closed at its bounded structured-parser scope by the post-merge acceptance audit, read back as completed. That closure covers the structured parser subset only: [#13](https://github.com/Marcus-Levin/hylja/issues/13) transformation, [#19](https://github.com/Marcus-Levin/hylja/issues/19) at a real send point, and every held-out, scored and enforcement claim in this paragraph remain unimplemented and unclaimed, and the preceding sentences still record what was outstanding *at that checkpoint*.

Second independent review (of `d74ee19`) requested changes on one newly introduced defect, F5, and one documentation nit, F6. F1–F4 were re-verified as fixed. F6 is corrected in the F1 paragraph above.

**F5 — a surrogate pair straddling a decode-slice boundary was silently corrupted, and it was reproduced here before it was fixed.** The slice-charged decoder added in `d74ee19` cut literal runs by UTF-16 index, and `TextEncoder` replaces each *unpaired* surrogate with U+FFFD, so a high surrogate landing on a slice boundary became two replacement characters while the parse still reported `COMPLETE` with no reason and no opaque range. The source comment claimed the opposite was impossible, and the differential oracle did not cover it: its astral shape was `'😀'.repeat(3000)` (6 000 units, one slice) and its generated runs were 1–14 units, so neither straddled an 8 192 boundary and the group passed on both the buggy head and the good one. Reproduced at this worktree: an astral character at run offset **8191** and **16383** decoded to `U+FFFD U+FFFD` here against the correct `U+1F600` at `79fbd2b`, identical in both slots and with `COMPLETE`/no reasons. Measured scope before the fix: divergence only when the high surrogate lands on a boundary, for every astral character tried, in both decoder slots, with escapes and `+` adjacent, and through `CONNECTION_STRING` (the same `parseUrl` path). The fix is one boundary rule: a slice end that would land on a high surrogate moves back one unit and the pair is encoded whole by the next slice, which always advances because a slice still ends at least one unit past its start. The source comment now says precisely what is guaranteed — the byte stream is the concatenation of the encoding of each code point, and the parser's pre-existing `INVALID_TEXT` entry guard already refuses input containing an unpaired surrogate, so a well-formed pair is the only thing a slice can split in practice.

**Closing the class, not the instance.** The oracle now sweeps **every** run offset across a whole decode-slice period (0…8194) with three astral characters — `U+1F600`, `U+10437`, `U+10FFFF` — in both decoder modes, plus a second period around 16 384 where a deferred pair meets the end of the run, and fixed cases at the boundaries with an escaped pair (`%F0%9F%98%80`) as well as a literal one, because the boundary is measured in input units and each escape adds three. That sweep uses a **single encode of the whole run** as its oracle — never a slice, so it is not circular — anchored to the strict per-code-point oracle at 30 sampled offsets so the cheap oracle cannot itself be subtly wrong; the strict per-code-point oracle still covers the small and medium shapes, the `+` and escape semantics, and every error path. The group asserts the sweep's own size, so a future edit cannot quietly shrink it back to one offset. An external differential probe over **164 016** comparisons (three astral characters at every offset across two full slice periods in both slots, astral beside `%41`, `+` and `%C3%A9`, back-to-back pairs, and a run made only of pairs) reports **0** mismatches against the strict algorithm at this head. Assertions on megabyte-scale shapes compare whole values but report only lengths and the differing code points, so a failure prints one readable line instead of kilobytes of payload. Cost: the decode-equivalence group now takes about 5 s of the file's 5.2 s, which is the price of a guard on a silent wrong-value class; it is bounded work with no sleep, no wall-clock assertion and no CPU stress.

**Red before green for F5, and the non-vacuity gap it closed.** Against the shipped `d74ee19` the new group fails with exactly the defect signature — expected `U+1F600`, actual `U+FFFD U+FFFD` at run offset 8191 — and 12 of 13 groups still pass. Removing the new boundary rule from this head's source fails the same group, so the guard is load-bearing rather than decorative. The F6 gap is now stated in the plan: the previous non-vacuity check was a single `+` mutation and said nothing about the astral path, which is precisely where the oracle was blind.

No production vault yet beyond minimal development fixture state. No unverified hosted semantic request, opaque protected content, or unchecked stream is silently released; this slice is not a production traffic claim.

Bounded, **NON-ENFORCING** #8 credential detectors exist in [`src/secret-detectors.ts`](../src/secret-detectors.ts). Format rules cover private-key blocks (PEM, PGP, SSH2, PuTTY) and common token prefixes (cloud, source-control, chat, payment, npm, JWT/JWE). Context rules cover:
- authorization, cookie and API-key headers;
- URL userinfo up to the last `@`;
- credential-like key assignments, including ones nested inside other values and `$`-sigil keys;
- CLI flags, Dockerfile `ENV`, `.netrc` after `machine`/`default`/`login` (same or earlier lines), `curl -u`, XML elements (including CDATA), key/value attribute and Kubernetes name/value pairs, token-only URL userinfo, YAML block scalars and trusted #7 field keys.

Context values over-cover to the end of the line or quote rather than stop inside a secret. Quoted values may span lines within a 64 KiB window, and an unterminated quote covers the whole window. Scanning resumes at the end of the opening line, so a key swallowed by a stray quote still gets its own candidate. A single quoted value longer than 64 KiB is covered only up to the window end. The detectors emit `CREDENTIAL_OR_SECRET` / `SECRET`, non-reversible v1 evidence with spans and optional domain-separated, tenant-keyed HMAC fingerprints, never values. Anyone holding the key can still test guesses of low-entropy values. Whole-value env/template references and explicit masks are skipped. Known gaps:
- no generic high-entropy detection, so unknown token formats without a credential-like key are missed;
- attached short flags (`-psecret`), prose (`the password is x`) and a `.netrc` `password x` without a preceding `machine`/`default`/`login` token are missed; keys that describe a credential (`password_hint`, `token_type`) are ignored;
- over-covering can span structural characters in inline contexts.

The synthetic golden check is a development measurement of detector recall, **not** held-out or zero-egress evidence; that needs #13 transformation and the #19 sentinel on actual outbound bytes. The direct detector API remains field-local; #6 composition scans its normalized views and applies trusted #7 field keys to parsed values.

Primary issues: #6-#13, #19, and #37 (PERSON/EMAIL/PHONE candidate source).

A bounded, **NON-ENFORCING** #37 candidate source exists in [`src/contact-candidates.ts`](../src/contact-candidates.ts). It emits v1 detector evidence and spans, without values, for synthetic EMAIL/PHONE patterns and tenant/project-scoped configured NAME dictionaries, and it rejects dotted IPv4/versions, ISO dates and timestamps, UUIDs, MACs and bare numeric IDs without a phone keyword. It deliberately biases toward recall: numeric ranges, ISBN-like and grouped digit runs are pinned, known over-cloaks, and a phone keyword before a dotted IP also over-cloaks. Dotted phone numbers without a keyword, email local parts over 64 characters, and domains with more than 16 labels are missed, and local parts containing rarer atext (`/ = ? & ~`) are truncated to the part after it. More than 256 candidates in one field fails closed (`FAILURE`) to match the v1 composer limit; hostile or very long logs can trigger it. Its direct API operates on caller-supplied text; #6 composition now also scans raw and folded normalized views with explicit offset provenance and composes #7 parsed field values under the same tenant/project scope. The direct API retains Unicode dashes, full-width digits, special case folds (`İ`, `ß`) and zero-width-character gaps when used alone. Results distinguish `COMPLETE` from `PARTIAL` (a requested name dictionary was invalid or out of scope) and `FAILURE`. It emits no sensitivity, so v1 composition stays unresolved until trusted configuration supplies one, which the unit composition below now accepts as a tenant/project-bound opaque configuration. Its synthetic golden check is a development measurement, **not** held-out recall or leak evidence; #37 stays open for held-out evaluation, the downstream #13/#19 text slice and independent review.

A bounded, **NON-ENFORCING** #19 egress sentinel core exists in [`src/egress-sentinel.ts`](../src/egress-sentinel.ts). It checks exact outbound bytes independently of the #8 detector stack:
- known planted originals and canaries, held only as keyed fingerprints. Long values are matched after separators are stripped, and short values against windows of whole consecutive tokens (so `Or la`, `o r l a` and `10.0.0.1 x` match, but not text inside a longer word). Both sides are folded for compatibility, case, marks, invisible characters, common Cyrillic/Greek/Armenian homoglyphs and leading zeros in every digit run. Keyed prefilters plus capped HMAC verifications and prefilter probes keep work bounded;
- its own high-risk pattern set, tested on raw and compatibility-normalized case-preserving text; credential assignments ignore references, type names, booleans, member expressions, a bare identifier that repeats its key (`{ password: password }`), whole quoted branch labels of a conditional (`? 'SECRET' : 'INTERNAL'`) and `PWD` values starting with `/` or `~`; URL passwords (including an empty user name, `redis://:password@host`) ignore only whole placeholders (`${password}`, `{{password}}`, `$DB_PASS`, `%s`, `<password>`);
- canonical views: #6 decoded views (including printable strings of binary decodes), its own escape decoding (JSON/JS/Python `\U`/octal/CSS/`%u`/HTML entities/quoted-printable) iterated to a bounded fixpoint, byte-valued views of contiguous escape runs and quoted Latin-1/byte strings, and printable-byte views of short encoded binary runs. When the scope registers short originals, a separate bounded, match-only pass checks Base64/hex alphabet runs of 6–15 characters (plus optional padding) and their tentative decompression; it does not add opaque-byte counts, though a signed nested container still blocks;
- a destination/profile assertion read once, and tenant-scoped handles that must be passed explicitly (`null` for unprotected egress).

The following all `BLOCK`:
- invalid UTF-8;
- uninspectable (#6 PARTIAL) content;
- a known original, canary or high-risk pattern found in any decoded view. Beyond #6 and the escape views, the sentinel rebuilds encoded data that was split up: Base64 or hex in chunks separated by whitespace (any amount, including indentation and pretty-printed JSON arrays), commas, quotes, brackets, JSON `\n`/`\r`/`\t` escapes or `:;|.&`, with plain words between chunks dropped; hex split by `-`, `_` or `/`; byte pairs (`63:6f`, `63 6f`, `0x63, 0x6f`, JSON/YAML arrays of pairs, `xxd -g1`/`xxd -i`/`hexdump -C`/`od` dumps, whose offset and ASCII columns are dropped, in both the canonical spacing and the spacing a sender chooses inside the hex field, since each row is read in every reading of its own hex field); and default `xxd` two-byte groups with offsets and ASCII columns. A prefix that misaligns chunks (a digest or word glued before them) is also tried without it. Escaped byte runs of four or more bytes (`%xx`, `\xNN`, `\u00NN`, octal, numeric HTML entities, quoted-printable and caret controls) and mixed quoted Latin-1/byte strings are also reconstructed, read across **one** separator character between two escapes, so an escape list is read as the bytes it spells and not as the formatting around it (`=1F =8B =08 …`, `%1f %8b …`, `\u001f \u008b …`); one run is read whole up to `MAX_ESCAPED_RUN` (32 KiB) and the rest as the next run, with the separator absorbed so the two stay adjacent and concatenate back into the same bytes. Reconstructions a representation declares about itself are rebuilt too: chunks labelled with a key (`part1: …`, `p1=…`, `part-1=…`, JSON and YAML lists, arrays of labelled JSON objects), chunks joined by a declared separator (` + `, ` / `, ` > `, written against either neighbour, line-broken, percent- or JSON-escaped), and decimal byte arrays including quoted numbers, trailing commas and Node `Buffer` JSON (`{"type":"Buffer","data":[…]}`). Out-of-range three-digit octal escapes block as uninspectable. Binary layers reached through these representations are decompressed: gzip/zlib with a signature in the first 16 bytes of a run, of the concatenation of runs or of a later run, or anywhere inside a run (first 256 signatures, for matching); brotli and raw deflate at the start, and by a bounded interior read of the runs the opaque count does not count (see the known limits for its exact ceiling). Nested layers are followed up to four deep and streams after a stream's end, including empty ones, along a chain of up to 256, within a 4 MiB per-message output budget. Text layers are matched and decoded further, including text found under brotli/raw-deflate output that may be accidental (decoded but not counted); binary layers are matched as printable bytes and as UTF-16 but not decoded further. This precise-reason guarantee is verified for the forms listed here, not for arbitrary custom encodings, and a message already blocked as opaque may report only `OPAQUE_EMBEDDED`;
- embedded opaque binary, as a **best-effort heuristic**, not a completeness claim. Container formats (zip, bzip2, xz, 7z, zstd) block outright when visible in encoded bytes or under a signed gzip/zlib layer. A container signature reached only through tentative brotli/raw-deflate decoding is not by itself an opaque-content finding; a successfully decoded signed gzip/zlib layer nested beneath it still blocks as opaque. More than 32 bytes in total per message, across all views, of binary that looks encoded blocks: long Base64/hex runs, clearly encoded short-chunk sequences, equal-group separated hex, binary gzip/zlib output and bytes after the end of a stream. Not counted: unbroken exact-length digests and 64-bit/object ids in hex; algorithm-matched exact-length digests in `shaNNN-`/`h1:` or digest context (SRI, go.sum, Content-MD5), plus MD5-length ETag values; UUIDs; structurally valid standard-Base64 SSH ed25519 public-key blobs; lines of a PEM certificate or public key whose body is one DER SEQUENCE of exactly its stated length (real or JSON-escaped line breaks), the Base64 alphabet, identifiers and paths made of words and numeric ids, decimal numbers, MAC addresses, and hex lists (fingerprints, hashes, ids). Random tokens and session ids block, since in protected egress they are credentials themselves (decision 009);
- oversize messages;
- an exhausted view or verification budget;
- errors and outage.

Payloads dense with long alphanumeric identifiers can exhaust #6's decode-work budget and therefore block. A streaming gate holds the complete message and releases nothing before the check. ALLOW returns a private copy of the checked bytes. Results and regression records carry codes and refs, never bytes or values.

Known limits:
- a short value glued into a longer word (`OrlaSmith`) is not matched; the short-encoding pass checks only whole Base64/hex alphabet runs of 6–15 characters (plus padding), when short originals are registered, and blocks if more than 8,192 distinct tokens need inspection;
- confusable coverage is a small table, not full UTS #39;
- only the listed escape families and a few named HTML entities are decoded;
- a bare short base64 DER body without a header or known value passes the pattern set;
- binary disguised as recognized digests or ids (hex runs of digest or id length, UUID-shaped) is not counted; a digest context exempts only the decoded length of its named algorithm. A syntactically valid SSH ed25519 public-key field still cannot prove that its 32 key bytes were not repurposed as opaque data. A PEM body only has to be DER-shaped, so random data behind a matching length header is not counted; base32 is not decoded; quoted-printable is decoded only in runs of two or more escapes;
- a random token that happens to split into word-like segments (roughly 0.1% of 30-byte Base64 tokens without padding) reads as an identifier and is not counted; comma-separated lists of short digit-heavy camelCase names (`UTF8ToUTF16LE, UTF16LEToUTF8, …`) can count as opaque;
- the advisory-suffix identifier exemption accepts only short path words (including capitalized, CamelCase and underscore-separated words) or numeric components of up to four digits; repeated slash components are treated as encoded data and never identifier-exempt beyond an optional leading URL `//`, with advisory or generic suffixes and after percent or Unicode escape decoding. Longer or mixed alphanumeric paths may block as opaque even when they are legitimate;
- the opaque-binary count misses uncompressed random data in several ordinary-looking shapes: Base64 in chunks of about 8 characters or fewer, hex chunk and byte-pair lists, chunks joined by `:;|.&`, and brotli/raw-deflate output that could be accidental. Such data passes unless it holds a known value or pattern;
- encoded data split into a declared form is rebuilt only **decode-only**: labelled chunks, separator joins and decimal byte arrays are read as text, decompressed, container-checked and matched, but they add no opaque bytes of their own, so an ordinary engineering numeric array, an RGB/matrix list or a labelled configuration list is not blocked by its shape. Compressed opaque data inside them still blocks (decompression output is counted) and a container signature inside them still blocks. Unknown *uncompressed* binary inside these forms passes; that is a declared limit, not a completeness claim;
- those reconstructions are bounded: at most 512 labelled fields or 256 separator chunks per group (a wider group is read in parts, and a truncated part still inflates its own head), 256 characters of filler between labelled fields, a 64 KiB joined value, a 24-character label, a 1 KiB value per field, a 32 KiB decimal array, chunks of at least two characters for separator joins, and evidence judged per value (at least half clearly encoded, one unambiguously). Label order is reconstructed for one numbered series at a time, so a group whose labels are not one series is not reordered as a whole, and a group split wider than the bounds above is not joined. A payload that needs more than 16,384 distinct reconstructions, more than 8 MiB of joined value or more than 8 MiB of joined characters in one message is uninspectable (`SENTINEL_BUDGET`), not clean;
- a labelled group is read as whole, as its clearly-encoded values, as its non-decimal values, as one declared label series at a time (`part1 … partN`, `p2=…`, or the keys of an array of labelled objects, `{"part":1,"data":"…"}`), as each of those in numeric-label order, as first-wins and last-wins where a series repeats a label number, and as the metadata beside its dominant series. Selecting by label name only is what keeps this linear: a group of n labelled fields yields at most n/2 series readings, each costing only its own size, so there is no subset search. A payload spread across two declared names *and* an interleaved unrelated field is still not reconstructed, because recovering it would mean choosing a subset of names; a payload spread across two declared names with no unrelated field is, since reading all values in text order already is that sequence;
- a label drawn from the hex alphabet is read only when its own numbers declare one complete series (`face1 … faceN`, or counted from 0), because a dump offset column carries numbers too and only a complete run tells the two apart. A hex-labelled group with a gap in its numbering, a repeated number, no first part, no numbers at all (`face: …`), one field, or parts that alternate between a hex-alphabet name and another name is therefore not reconstructed: with the planted originals registered, all seven of those spellings release the note with `release` at 8-character chunk width, as they did at `5edaffc`. The last of them is the two-name case above in a different spelling, and closing it would mean choosing a subset of names; the rest are the price of not reading an offset column as a payload;
- encoded data is not rebuilt from escaped bytes split across labels longer than 24 characters, from a decimal array longer than 32 KiB, or from a separator join whose chunks are one character long. **Qualified in place 2026-10-02 (issue #126):** escapes split across the fields of one **declared label series** are rebuilt, and the same reconstruction reads `part1: \x63\x6f…` in every supported family and in the `{"part":1,"data":"…"}` spelling. What stays unbuilt is the `=>` separator between two escapes (a run of separator-class characters still needs no other character in it), a payload spread across **two** declared names — closing that would mean choosing a subset of names — and a series whose fields are not all escape lists; **Qualified again in place 2026-10-03:** a hex-alphabet-labelled escape series (`face1: \x1f`) is excluded from that reading and is **not** rebuilt, which is stricter than the chunk reader beside it rather than a parity; a label directly preceded by a backslash is not read either. Both are published gaps, not completeness claims.
- a plain chunk sequence interrupted by unrelated labelled lines (`part2: x` between two halves of a Base64 payload) is not re-joined across the interruption unless the interrupted fields are one declared label series, which is read without the interruption; the labelled values and each contiguous run are still read separately, and the interrupted fields are also read as one group. This is the same limit as the interrupted-sequence entries above, not a completeness claim;
- escaped bytes split across labels or punctuation, bare escape runs shorter than four bytes, unsupported mixed escape syntax, and multiply serialized Latin-1 strings may be blocked as opaque without a precise known-original finding. Unicode and HTML escapes of ordinary accented prose are inspected but do not add to opaque-byte counts unless they contain control bytes; this can miss binary made solely of high Latin-1 bytes. Opaque counting remains best-effort. **Qualified in place 2026-10-02:** a *single* separator character between two escapes in **ordinary text** is now read (space, tab, CR, LF, `,;:|.&`, quotes, brackets, or a JSON `\n`/`\r`/`\t`) and a separated escape list is reconstructed, decompressed and counted as a contiguous one is, added to — never instead of — the contiguous reading. **Inside a quoted string (`b'…'`, a JSON string) every literal character is a byte of that string and no separator is skipped there**, so a compressed payload whose own bytes spell a separator-class character keeps them; what remains of this bullet is a split of **two or more** separator characters or a split across labels and other punctuation, which still releases the payload for every family except `\x` (the byte-pair reader already rebuilds that spelling at a single separator). **Two of the three are superseded by #126 below**: a bounded separator run of two or more characters is read, and escapes spread across one declared label series are read; a gap wider than `MAX_ESCAPE_SEPARATOR` or one holding any other character, and a payload spread across two declared names, still release it except for `\x`. The third is a signed stream that starts inside one 32 KiB run and continues in the next, which the interior scan reads run by run and which is therefore refused by the opaque count rather than inflated, and the case where the added reading is itself a corrupted stream whose decode raises the opaque total in the same view: an opaque message stops further rounds by design, so the precise finding a later round would have reported can be replaced by `OPAQUE_EMBEDDED` (one case in 522 in the differential below, never a release). The surviving limits are measured and pinned in `test/egress-sentinel.test.mjs`; #88 stays open for them and for the wider items;
- brotli and raw-deflate streams are found at the start of a decoded run, of the concatenation or of a stream's remainder, at any gzip/zlib signature inside a run (first 256 signatures, for matching), and — because neither format carries a signature — by a bounded interior read of the runs the opaque count does not count: a run whose exact byte length is a digest length, a short run that is not a recognized digest, id or public key, and a run inside a DER-shaped public PEM body. Brotli/raw-deflate output is tentative: it and nested layers are inspected for known values and patterns, but cannot independently raise or lower the opaque-byte count; a short unsigned stream holding unknown opaque data can pass. **The interior read is bounded in two different ways, because the two kinds of exempt run are not the same claim.** A run whose exemption is only a coincidence of length — a bare digest-length run that nothing declared — is *obliged*: `MAX_INTERIOR_BYTES` (32 KiB of such run bytes per message) is read in full or the message is uninspectable (`SENTINEL_BUDGET`). Without that refusal a bounded read that ran out released whatever it never reached, and adding harmless digest lines *before* a hidden payload disabled detection of it; that was the blocking finding of the independent review of this feature and it is now closed, with the fix measured against every decoy count rather than one. Every other exempt run — a short run, a declared reconstruction, a digest the message named under an algorithm context (`sha256-`, `h1:`, `Content-MD5:`, `ETag:`), a public certificate body — keeps the exemption this module has always published for digests and public certificates: it is read within `MAX_OPTIONAL_WINDOWS` (64) windows and exhausting that is never a reason to refuse, because refusing it would refuse ordinary `go.sum` and certificate traffic, which is what the exemption is for. Distinct run values only: a list repeats its entries, and reading the same bytes twice is work nobody can be billed for twice. Measured on this host at the head that closed the finding: one compressed planted note in a 64-byte digest-shaped hex run blocks with both findings at 0, 1, 4, 8, 32 and 200 preceding decoy runs of either the sha256 or the sha512 spelling and under `go.sum`-shaped decoys, decoy-heavy counts beyond the byte ceiling return a reason and no `release` bytes, and the module's own controls — 1,000 bare hex digests, 2,000 `sha256-` digests, 300 `go.sum` lines, 200 trace ids, a public certificate — stay allowed inside their work bounds;
- a hex-dump row is read in every spelling of its hex field, not only in the canonical one. The complete row (every cell hex of the row's own width) and the row cut at the first run of two or more spaces (the unmarked ASCII column `xxd` and `hexdump -C` pad before) are both rebuilt, so one sender-controlled space, tab or removed `|` marker no longer truncates the reconstruction to the first group and a complete reading is never displaced by a shorter one. Two readings are the bound: a row whose own cells are split into cells of a different width (`1f 8b` inside a two-byte-group row) is read only up to the width change, and the offset column is always dropped. **The ASCII column is dropped whether or not the row marks it** (`xxd`, `hexdump -C` and `od` write one after `  |`, but a sender can remove it), and it is the row's own offsets that make that possible: consecutive dump rows advance the offset by exactly one row, so the row width is knowable without trusting where the column separator is. A doubled space or tab *inside* the hex field adds no cell and leaves the row's offsets and cell count untouched, so the complete reading still spans the field; the column's cells never are read, at any column width — two-character or four-character hex cells, two-character or four-character words, or dots. That matters because reading them interleaved the column into the reconstruction, destroyed the stream it carried, and released a planted original and canary with `release` bytes where the base blocked with both precise findings; every one of those column spellings is now blocked precisely, marked or unmarked, and ordinary dumps carrying them are `ALLOW` again. A block whose offsets do not advance uniformly — a partial or hand-written listing — has no agreed row width and is read as before, which is a declared limit, not a completeness claim;
- a single short default-`xxd` row is not rebuilt; two or more rows with at least 16 reconstructed bytes are required, and uncompressed opaque data in hex lists remains a count gap;
- credential assignments flag bare identifiers other than the key itself (`secret: required`, `Password: pass`), because unquoted passwords look the same; a `PWD` value starting with `/` or `~` is never flagged, whatever follows;
- a 1 MiB adversarial message of many small compressed streams as hex pairs can take about 4 s to check;
- process memory is still **not** bounded by the sentinel's own budgets, and the reason is in the runtime rather than in this module. A synchronous zlib call allocates one output buffer per call at Node's default `chunkSize` of 16 KiB, and a call that decodes nothing keeps that buffer until the runtime can run again: measured here, a synchronous batch of 24,000 failed `inflateRawSync` calls on a 64-byte buffer raises `arrayBuffers` by 16,148 bytes each, by 4,037 at `chunkSize` 4,096 and by 1,049 at 1,024, independent of `windowBits`. **That is a high-water mark, not a per-process retention**: after two event-loop turns and a forced GC, `arrayBuffers` and `external` return to within 0.1 MiB of their baseline while RSS does not come back, so RSS is not evidence of retained reachable objects, and no permanent-leak claim is made here. Every decode attempt in this module therefore names its own `DECODE_CHUNK` (1 KiB), which costs nothing measurable on successful decodes (median of five, 1 MiB gunzip: 1.92 ms against 1.49 ms at Node's default) and makes a synchronous decoy-signature scan's high-water mark sixteen times smaller: the same 60 KiB decoy-signature payload shows about 20 MiB without it and about 1 MiB with it, pinned by test as a work bound. What this module does not bound: a long synchronous batch of many messages keeps raising the high-water mark by its chunk per attempt and RSS does not come back, so a process checking many messages holds more resident memory than its declared work ceilings suggest, and `--max-old-space-size` does not bound it (a 192 MiB cap still showed three to four times that in RSS on a finished check). Measured on the 60 pre-existing groups whose test text is byte-identical to the base, one locked run each: base `e6f1a46` 1.92 GiB, this feature's earlier head 4.16 GiB, the final head 2.83 GiB — the chunk change removes about a third, and the multiplier that remains is the interior read's own attempts, which is bounded by `MAX_INTERIOR_BYTES` but costs about 2.8 s and a non-decaying ~200 MB of high-water mark on an *allowed* 1,000-line bare-hex digest list, against about 0.5 s and a decaying ~74 MB on the same message at the base. That cost is this module's, not the runtime's, and it is the price of reading every starting offset of every obliged run;
- byte arrays that spell a container signature (`0x50, 0x4b, 0x03, 0x04, …`) block, including this module's own signature table;
- unquoted dotted credential values (`password=Synthetic.Passw0rd`) read as member expressions and are not flagged by the assignment pattern;
- short values match on whole-token windows, so prose like `or la` can match a short entry `Orla` and block.
- dense source text can exhaust #6's decode budget and block (seen on 2,000-character chunks of this repository's own detector sources). **Not yet integrated**: no adapter calls it at a real send point, #13 transformation does not exist, the #11 judgment seam exists but is a local/synthetic-only non-enforcing module that nothing calls and that has no external path (see the #11 paragraphs below), and the #37-to-egress text slice has not run end to end. Seeded-leak tests are synthetic unit evidence, not a release claim.

**Escape-wrapped byte lists separated by one character — one bounded #88 residual, dated 2026-10-02 (issue #88 stays open).** Reproduced on base `e2b5714`, not taken from the issue summary: the two escape readers in `escapedByteRuns` consumed *contiguous* escapes, so the first separator ended a run and every remaining escape was a run of its own below the four-byte floor. Measured in process against that base with synthetic data only, across the supported families with one separator character between escapes — space, tab, CR, LF, `,`, `;`, `:`, `|`, `&`, `.` or a JSON `\n`/`\r`/`\t`, in raw text and in a JSON body: a gzip, zlib, raw-deflate or brotli note carrying a planted original and a canary returned `ALLOW` **with `release` bytes** for `=XX` (quoted-printable, either case), `%xx`, `\u00NN`, `\NNN` and `&#xNN;`/`&#NN;`, and 96 bytes of synthetic opaque binary under the same spellings were never counted and were also `ALLOW`ed with `release` bytes; the same *plaintext* note written as separated escapes was released for the percent, octal, quoted-printable and lowercase-QP spellings. Only two cases blocked: `\xNN`, which the byte-pair reader already rebuilds at a single separator, and percent escapes inside a JSON string, where the quoted reader read the separator as a literal byte and so blocked with `OPAQUE_EMBEDDED` but never the precise finding. The fix is one reader rule plus one bound: **exactly one** separator character between two escapes is skipped and dropped from the reconstruction, by the direct reader and inside a quoted string alike (the closing quote is never one, so a run cannot continue past its own string), and one run is read whole up to the new `MAX_ESCAPED_RUN` (32 KiB) with the separator after the bound absorbed into the finished run's span, so the next run starts adjacent to it and the two concatenate back into the same bytes — chosen below #6's own `MAX_RUN` so that a run this module builds is never a view #6 has to refuse, which is what made the dense `\u0041 ` timing input uninspectable without it. The four-byte floor, the supported families, that one-character separator and that bound are the whole of this reader's grammar; no detection threshold, opaque count, interior-read ceiling, #6 budget or test/process budget moved. **Corrected in place 2026-10-02 after independent review of `c091789`:** this paragraph originally claimed every pre-existing escaped positive, negative and budget refusal was unchanged, which was false — the first version of the separator rule was also applied inside the quoted-string reader, which changed pre-existing escaped positives in both decision and reason code (see the next paragraph). Evidence: five new groups in `test/egress-sentinel.test.mjs` (a family x separator x container x compression matrix, the opaque-count group with ordinary-text controls, a fixed-seed generated sweep, a bounded-work and bound-straddling group, and a group that pins the remaining limits), **RED on base `e2b5714` at 5 failed / 72 passed of 77 and 77/77 on this head**; a differential sweep of 21 ordinary repository documents and 45 ordinary log/prose samples carrying `=NN`, `#NN`, `0xNN` and percent-shaped tokens gave identical decisions on base and on this head, and the six adversarial messages of that pre-existing bounded-work group measure within noise of the base on an idle host (492/609/967/612/901/538 ms there against 516/619/1036/589/800/574 ms here, the `\u0041 ` one now blocked as `OPAQUE_EMBEDDED` instead of allowed). Full locked sequence on this head (Node 22.22.3, npm 10.9.8): `npm ci --ignore-scripts --no-fund` 0, `npm run check:fixtures` 0, `npm run build` 0, `npm run typecheck` 0, `npm run check:release` 0 (17 synthetic cases), `npm test` 0 at **860/860** (0 skipped) and `npm run test:coverage` 0 at **860/860**, with compiled `egress-sentinel.js` at **99.65 % line / 96.47 % branch / 100.00 % function** and all files at **98.74 / 93.14 / 96.84** (base `e2b5714`: 99.63 / 96.43 / 100.00 and 98.70 / 93.10 / 96.81, the same three uncovered defence-in-depth sites); one earlier locked coverage run on this same head reported **859/860** because the pre-existing load-sensitive wall-clock group `review: shared-prefix entries and adversarial payloads stay within a bounded-work budget` exceeded its own 5 s threshold under coverage instrumentation (`jo x jo x jo 5002.9ms`), and that group fails the same way on the unmodified base in this environment (10.4 s under coverage there, passing without instrumentation), so it is the timing sensitivity this plan already records rather than a behaviour change; `npm run audit:deps` 0, `npm run --silent sbom` + `check:sbom` 0 (`DEPENDENCY_EVIDENCE_CONSISTENT`), `node evaluations/preparation-integrity.mjs` 0 as `PREPARATION_VALID_BUT_NOT_ELIGIBLE / PUBLIC_DRAFT_ONLY`, `git diff --check` clean. The wider #88 items are untouched by this: opaque counting of uncompressed random data in short chunks, hex lists and `:;|.&` joins, the bare-identifier and digit-heavy camelCase false positives, the #6 decode budget on dense source chunks, the ~4 s worst-case 1 MiB cost, the absence of a whole-process RSS cap, and the absence of any adapter at a real send point. This module still authenticates nobody and sends no bytes.

**Review fix, dated 2026-10-02: the separator rule belongs to ordinary text, not to a quoted string.** An independent review of `c091789` returned REQUEST_CHANGES with one blocker and one documentation finding, both reproduced here from the public interface with synthetic data only, not from the reviewer's own artefacts. **The blocker was a fail-open regression of my own making.** `c091789` skipped the separator character inside the quoted-string reader as well; inside `b'…'` or a JSON string every literal character is a byte of that string, so a compressed payload whose own bytes spell a separator-class character, written literally between two escapes, lost that byte. Measured on `c091789` over a 522-case base-versus-head differential (four compressors, three notes, six spellings, thirteen separator characters, raw/JSON/Python-literal shapes): **6 cases returned `ALLOW` with `release` bytes where the base returned `BLOCK` with the precise known-original and canary findings**, and **232 cases lost the precise finding** (a `b'…'` literal of a contiguous gzip or brotli stream reported only `OPAQUE_EMBEDDED`, because a printable character inside the literal — a space in the note — was dropped too). The reviewer's own reproducer is included in the regression set verbatim in mechanism: a gzip note containing a tab, the tab written literally between two percent escapes inside a JSON string and inside a Python byte literal, which base blocks with both findings and `c091789` released with 306 and 295 bytes. **The fix is one rule plus one reading.** The separator rule now applies to ordinary text only, where it is a statement about the *text between* escapes; the quoted reader is byte-for-byte the base's again, so no byte of a string is ever dropped and every quoted reading this module had is intact. Ordinary text is read **twice** — the contiguous reading first, the separated one added to it and never instead of it, so a sender who formats an escape list and a sender whose separator character is a byte of the payload both keep every reading they had — and a separated run that skipped nothing is not yielded twice. `MAX_ESCAPED_RUN` stays in the direct reader only, where the absorbed separator keeps consecutive runs adjacent; the quoted string is read whole again, as before. **Measured on this head** (`/tmp/hylja-probe`, three builds in one process, synthetic data only): over the 522-case differential, **0 cases are released where the base blocked**, 7 cases the base released are now blocked as `OPAQUE_EMBEDDED` (the closure this slice exists for), 52 cases gain `OPAQUE_EMBEDDED` on top of the base's findings, 2 keep the known-original finding and lose the later canary finding, and **exactly 1 case loses a precise finding** — the added reading is itself a corrupted stream, its decode raises the opaque total in the same view, and an opaque message stops further rounds by design, so the message is refused as `OPAQUE_EMBEDDED` rather than by name. Over the 360-case quoted-literal regression corpus the two builds agree exactly on precision (json 109/120, python 109/120, unquoted 54/120 on both; `c091789` reported 42/120, 42/120, 42/120). Ordinary content is unchanged: **191 repository documents** (every tracked `.md`, `.ts`, `.mjs`, `.json`, `.yml`) and 21 documents plus 45 synthetic log/prose samples with `=NN`, `%NN`, `#NN`, `0xNN`, `&#NN;`, `\xNN`, ANSI, MIME and URL-shaped tokens give **identical decisions** on the base and on this head. Two new groups pin both halves (`a literal separator character between two escapes inside a quoted string is data`, `ordinary text with escape-shaped tokens and quoted literals is unchanged`): **RED at `c091789` (2 failed / 77 passed of 79, `json precise 56/160` against a floor of 100 and a `b'…'` literal reporting only `OPAQUE_EMBEDDED`)**, GREEN at 79/79 on this head, and RED again against the task base `e2b5714` at 7 failed / 72 passed of 79 for the whole slice. Nothing else moved: no detection threshold, opaque count, interior-read ceiling, `#6` budget or test/process budget; the six adversarial messages of the pre-existing bounded-work group measure within noise of the base again (479/558/900/544/743/568 ms here against 492/609/967/612/901/538 ms on the base, the `\u0041 ` one blocked as `OPAQUE_EMBEDDED` instead of allowed), and a dense 1 MiB escaped list decides in 0.7 s. The remaining limits are the ones the known-limits bullet above now states: a split of two or more separator characters, escapes split across labels and punctuation, a signed stream straddling the 32 KiB run bound, the opaque short-circuit described here, and #88's wider items. Issue #88 stays open and this module remains non-enforcing: no adapter calls it at a real send point and it authenticates nobody. Full locked sequence on this head (Node 22.22.3, npm 10.9.8, every costly run under `flock /tmp/hylja-agentic-campaign/test.lock`): `npm ci --ignore-scripts --no-fund` 0, `npm run check:fixtures` 0, `npm run build` 0, `npm run typecheck` 0, `npm run check:release` 0 (17 synthetic cases), `npm test` 0 at **862/862** (0 skipped), `npm run test:coverage` 0 at **862/862** with compiled `egress-sentinel.js` at **99.65 % line / 96.49 % branch / 100.00 % function** and all files at **98.75 / 93.16 / 96.85** (`c091789`: 99.65 / 96.47 / 100.00 and 98.74 / 93.14 / 96.84; the three uncovered sites are the same defence-in-depth paths: the `isDigest` Base64 length list and the two stream-gate catch blocks), `npm run audit:deps` 0, `npm run --silent sbom` + `check:sbom` 0 (`DEPENDENCY_EVIDENCE_CONSISTENT`), `node evaluations/preparation-integrity.mjs` 0 as `PREPARATION_VALID_BUT_NOT_ELIGIBLE / PUBLIC_DRAFT_ONLY`, `git diff --check` clean; both RED checkpoints are saved outside the repository (`/tmp/hylja-probe/red-reviewed-head.txt`: this test file against a build of `c091789`, 2 failed / 77 passed of 79; `/tmp/hylja-probe/red-base-with-review-fixes.txt`: the same file against a build of the task base `e2b5714`, 7 failed / 72 passed of 79). The remaining limits are the ones the known-limits bullet above now states:

**Review fix for #126, dated 2026-10-03: the declared escape series waives nothing, and one label-bound hole it had is closed.** An independent review of `0724426` returned REQUEST_CHANGES with one blocking finding, reproduced here from the public interface with synthetic data only and not from the reviewer's artefacts. **The blocker was a release of embedded opaque bytes on the reading this slice added.** The text a declared escape series spells was routed into a brand-new view marked `derived: true`, and in this module `derived` is the switch that turns off opaque-byte accounting for a **whole view** — both for the runs inside it and for what a decompression under it produces. Measured at `0724426`: `gzip` of 96 synthetic bytes, spelled as Base64 text one, two or three escapes per labelled field, was returned `ALLOW` with **1971 / 1270 / 1062 released bytes**, while the identical bytes were refused `OPAQUE_EMBEDDED` as plain text, as one ordinary separated escape list, as a labelled chunk series and at four escapes per field (where the direct reader's four-byte floor happens to fire). That contradicts the module's own contract ("any embedded opaque binary … is BLOCK") and the published invariant for every declared form ("compressed opaque data inside them still blocks"), and the earlier paragraph recorded the opposite. The base `7514bbc` released the same bytes in the same shape, so this was **not a regression** — the fix is the required narrowing, not a repair of a new release. **The fix is one per-run mechanism instead of a view-wide one.** `derived` is now what it says elsewhere and stays a property of a *whole decode*; the series' own text is exempted by `View.ownText`, the exact strings that view was built from, and only a run whose whole value is one of them is exempt from the byte count. Everything inside that text — a Base64 or hex payload, a signed stream's output, a container signature — raises the opaque total exactly as it does in a non-derived view. **A second, narrower hole was found while fixing the first, and it was in my own reader.** In a JSON body the line break between two labelled fields is the two characters `\n`, and the label scan started at its `n`: every field after the first was read as `npart2 … npart160`, so the one series was 159 fields whose join was the payload **minus its own first byte**. That join decoded to nothing, so the message was released while the same payload in every other spelling was refused. A label may no longer start inside a backslash escape, and the escape spelling is consumed before the label instead. **Measured at this head** (Node 22.22.3, npm 10.9.8): the reviewed head `0724426` is **RED at 1 failed / 84 passed of 85** on the new group `issue 126 review: a declared escape series waives no opaque byte count` (`base64/1: 'ALLOW' !== 'BLOCK'`) and this head is **85/85**; the task base `7514bbc` is RED at **5 failed / 80 passed of 85** for the whole slice. Every spelling of the reviewer's payload now refuses `OPAQUE_EMBEDDED` with no released bytes, in all eight families, at 1, 2, 3, 4 and 8 escapes per field, raw and in a JSON body. The own-shape exemption is unchanged: 60 fields spelling `ABAB…` stay `ALLOW` in the series spelling while the identical bytes as one ordinary separated list are refused by the pre-existing chunk reader, at the base as here, and both halves are now pinned so neither can be swapped for the other. Two measured differentials: **3,456 cases** (4 compressors x 8 families x 20 gaps x 3 containers, plus 6 declared-series shapes x 3 bytes-per-field over a note and an opaque stream) show **0 base-BLOCK-to-head-ALLOW cases and 0 lost precise findings**, 1,115 newly blocked; and the ordinary-content differential is unchanged at **589/590 identical decisions with 0 newly `ALLOW`ed**, the single difference being the reason a document blocks either way (`test/parser-time-budget.test.mjs`). The false-positive direction recorded in the paragraph below is unchanged by this fix. **Also corrected, non-blocking:** the escape series reading excludes hex-alphabet labels (`face1: \x1f`), which is **stricter** than the chunk reader beside it rather than the parity the earlier comment claimed; that gap is now stated where the reading is defined, pinned in the hex-series limits group (a hex-labelled escape series releases the note at one escape per field, as it does at the base, while the chunk spelling of the same bytes blocks), and listed with the remaining limits below. What remains, measured and pinned rather than claimed closed: a gap wider than `MAX_ESCAPE_SEPARATOR` or holding any other character, a payload across two declared names, a series whose fields are not all escape lists, **a hex-alphabet-labelled escape series**, unknown uncompressed binary inside a declared form (the module's decode-only limit, unchanged), brotli and raw-deflate output inside one of these shapes (the published tentative-output limit), a label directly preceded by a backslash, and a field straddling `MAX_ESCAPED_RUN` refused by the opaque count rather than reported by name. **Not claimed here:** independent exact-head approval of this head (the reviewed SHA has changed), hosted CI, utility measurement, or any authenticated send point — no adapter calls this module and it authenticates nobody. The full locked sequence and both RED checkpoint locations are in the delivery report, outside this repository.

**Escape-wrapped bytes across a wider gap, and across one declared label series — the bounded #126 follow-up, dated 2026-10-02 (issue #88 stays open; #126 is a scoped child of it).** Two shapes the paragraph above published as remaining were reproduced here from the public interface with synthetic data only, on the assigned base `7514bbc`, and both are now read. **(i) A gap wider than one character between two escapes.** `escapeSeparatorEnd` accepted a single separator-class character, or the two-character JSON spelling of one line break, so `=41, =42`, `%1f  %8b`, `\u001f\t \u008b`, a CRLF line-broken list and a JSON array of escapes (`"%1f", "%8b"`) each ended the run and every remaining escape became a one-byte run below the four-byte floor. Measured on that base over four compressors x eight escape families x the raw, `{"content":…}` and messages-body containers: a note carrying a planted original and a canary was returned **`ALLOW` with `release` bytes** for the quoted-printable (either case), octal, `\u00NN` and `\_`-adjacent spellings, and no family reported the precise finding. **(ii) Escapes distributed across one declared label series.** `part1: \x1f`, `part2: \x8b`, … was read as ordinary text: the labelled-field reader takes chunk values (`[A-Za-z0-9+/_-]`) and each escape was a one-byte run, so every shape of it — the lines, the `&`-joined query string, the `{"part":N,"data":"…"}` array and the non-numbered `data:` series — returned `ALLOW` with `release` bytes at the base, for every family and every compressor. The fix is **two added readings and no change to any earlier one**. The separator is now a **bounded run**: up to `MAX_ESCAPE_SEPARATOR` (8) characters, every one of them a separator-class character or a JSON line-break spelling, ending at the first character that opens the next escape (`&` is both a separator and the lead of `&#xNN;`, so a greedy scan would eat the next entity's lead and fail). It is still ordinary-text-only and still *added to* the contiguous reading, never instead of it; `quoted()` is byte-for-byte unchanged, so a literal separator-class character inside a quoted string is still data. The second reading rebuilds one escape list per **declared label series** — the fields sharing a label name, numbered or not — grouped and charged exactly as the chunk reader's series are (`MAX_DECLARED_FIELDS`, `labelledGap`, the per-message `DeclaredBudget`), with the same `MAX_ESCAPED_RUN` split, whose pieces keep the field's own label so the join reassembles a straddling field byte for byte. It joins one series and never a subset of names, skips no word, and adds no opaque bytes **of its own** (so labelled configuration cannot be blocked by the shape of its values) — what the text it spells **encodes** still counts, which the review fix below makes a per-run exemption rather than a view-wide one. Its own text goes to a view of its own rather than the shared joined text, because a series spelling all-hex letters (`=41 =42 …` -> `ABCDEF…`) would otherwise be re-read there as an encoded run and refused for the sender's own text; the exemption is exactly the strings that view was built from, and it is **corrected in place by the review fix below**, which found that a view-wide `derived` flag also waived what the text encodes. **Measured at this head** (Node 22.22.3, npm 10.9.8): the test file against a build of the assigned base `7514bbc` is **RED at 4 failed / 80 passed of 84** and **84/84** here, with the four failures exactly the new behaviour groups; over a 190-document + 400-sample ordinary-content differential (every tracked `.md`/`.ts`/`.mjs`/`.json`/`.yml` in this repository plus generated logs, configuration, JSON bodies, query strings and code) base and head agree on **589 of 590** decisions with **0 in the release direction**, and the single difference is a reason change on a document that blocks either way: `test/parser-time-budget.test.mjs` is `UNINSPECTED_CONTENT` at the base and `HIGH_RISK_PATTERN,OPAQUE_EMBEDDED` here, because a 500-character source slice carrying three thousand repeated percent escapes now assembles across `', '` into one counted run. That is the false-positive direction of this change and it is stated rather than hidden: **a long list of escape-shaped tokens separated only by separator-class characters can now cross `OPAQUE_BYTES`**, which is a utility/security trade-off like #88's other published false positives and not a neutral one. What remains, measured and pinned rather than claimed closed: a gap wider than `MAX_ESCAPE_SEPARATOR` or holding any other character (`- `, ` -> `, ` and `), a payload across two declared names, a series whose fields are not all escape lists, unknown uncompressed binary inside a declared form (the module's decode-only limit, unchanged), brotli and raw-deflate output inside one of these shapes (the published tentative-output limit: unsigned streams cannot raise the opaque count, so opaque bytes inside them still pass), and a straddling field refused by the opaque count rather than reported by name. **Not claimed here:** independent exact-head review, hosted CI, utility measurement, or any authenticated send point — no adapter calls this module and it authenticates nobody. The full locked sequence and the RED checkpoint location are in the delivery report, outside this repository.

**Both suite commands now schedule one test file at a time (2026-10-02, package-only; authored on base `e2b5714`, merged onto main `b9bf35e`).** Several suites in this repository assert wall-clock ceilings rather than injected clocks, including the per-payload 5 000 ms ceilings of the `shared-prefix entries and adversarial payloads stay within a bounded-work budget` group, so the number of this repository's own test **files** running at once is itself a variable those ceilings are measured against. `package.json` therefore adds `--test-concurrency=1` to `npm test` and to `npm run test:coverage`, and changes nothing else: same glob and file list, same discovery, same cases, same coverage instrumentation, same fixture guard, same build, and every budget, timing and resource assertion **byte-identical**. The option is the pinned runtime's own, read from `node --test --help` on the pinned Node 22.22.3 (`--test-concurrency=... specify test runner concurrency`) and confirmed by a four-file scheduling probe run outside the repository: unscheduled, all four file processes started together (844 ms wall for four 700 ms files), `--test-concurrency=1` ran them strictly one at a time (3 137 ms), `--test-concurrency=2` two at a time (1 610 ms). **Measured once each on the integrated state** — this branch merged onto main `b9bf35e` (PR #124 issue #88 escaped byte streams, PR #125 issue #123 prescreen distribution identity, PR #122 issue #114 XML overlap rewrite tests), the one `docs/plan.md` conflict resolved with every merged checkpoint kept, and the resulting tree differing from main in `package.json` alone: Node 22.22.3, npm 10.9.8, 12-core host, every run under `flock /tmp/hylja-agentic-campaign/test.lock` shared with other campaign sessions, host not verified idle, no load induced, nothing re-run to collect a pass — `npm run build` 0, `npm run typecheck` 0, `npm run check:release` 0 (17 synthetic cases), `npm run check:fixtures` 0, `npm test` 0 at **871/871** (0 fail, 0 skipped, 0 cancelled) in 166.4 s, and `npm run test:coverage` 0 at **871/871** in 230.1 s with `all files` **98.76 % line / 93.13 % branch / 96.86 % function** and `egress-sentinel.js` at 99.65 / 96.65 / 100.00; that bounded-work group measured 4 259 ms uninstrumented and 8 610 ms under coverage against its **unchanged** 5 000 ms per-payload ceiling, which was not raised, waived or relaxed, and the group passed in both runs. The first `check:fixtures` attempt failed with `unable to inspect Git index` because the merge was still unresolved in the index, a state that guard rejects by design; it passed on the staged resolution and nothing else was re-run. Those runs are of the merged tree, this paragraph was added after them, and no check reads `docs/plan.md`. **Preserved from the pre-integration head `a4cd5a8` (configuration `787a8f2`, base `e2b5714`) and not re-measured here:** `npm test` **855/855** in 156.4 s, `npm run test:coverage` **855/855** in 214.3 s at the same `all files` figures, focused `node --test --test-concurrency=1 --experimental-test-coverage test/egress-sentinel.test.mjs` **72/72** in 133.9 s, the unscheduled base `npm test` at 855/855 in 113.7 s, the discovery-equivalence check (the sorted set of executed test names identical, 853, across the unscheduled base, the scheduled head and the scheduled coverage log) and the heaviest bounded-work payload, `'jo x '.repeat(200_000)`, at 2 015.7 ms instrumented against that same 5 000 ms ceiling; the mixed-tree observation recorded there (870/870) substituted the then-unmerged #127 draft [`test/overlap-composition-draft.test.mjs`](../test/overlap-composition-draft.test.mjs), a file committed at `e2b5714` and since extended by #122, so the integrated run above used main's committed version of that file rather than the substitution. **No same-tree before/after is claimed for the wall clock**: 113.7 s is the unscheduled figure on the pre-merge suite and 166.4 s the scheduled figure on the merged suite, so that difference is scheduling plus suite growth and the isolated scheduling cost is the four-file probe above; the coverage run's 230 s also sits inside a CI job whose `timeout-minutes: 10` covers install, build, typecheck, `check:release`, audit and SBOM as well, which is root's to weigh. **No RED is claimed and none exists**: no contract changed, so the load-sensitive bounded-work failures recorded above and in the unmerged #127 report stay **unreproduced** by this slice, and one extra or differently shaped test file is **not** shown to have caused any of them; serializing removes contention between this repository's own files only — it bounds no busy or slower host, certifies no production latency and is not an SLA. No source file, threshold, budget, fixture, case, dependency or CI step was changed; hosted CI at the merged head, an independent review of that exact head, human merge approval and root's publication remain outstanding.
